Lemma 11.5. The ﬁbers of p: YK →K/G are described as follows:
• p is an isomorphism over (K/G)o;
• p is a P1-bundle over (Wσ/G)o, for each σ ∈G\G1;
• p is a P1 × P1-bundle over (Vσ,σ ′/G)o;
• The ﬁber of p over each point Zσ,σ ′,σ ′′/G is P1 × P1 × P1.
In particular, all the strata of p are relevant with irreducible ﬁbers, hence with trivial local systems.
Proof. This is essentially [Flo24, Proposition 4.2]. The ﬁrst assertion is clear. The ﬁbers of p over
points (Wσ/G)o are P1 since K/G has transversal A1-singularity along (Wσ/G)o. Around a point
P ∈(Vσ,σ ′/G)o, K/G is isomorphic to the germ of (C2/−1)2 × C2 at the origin in such a way that the
germ at P of (Vσ,σ ′/G)o is identiﬁed with the germ (0,0) × C2. Hence the resolution p, which is the
blow-up along the singular locus, is analytically locally isomorphic to the following germ
(Bl0(C2/−1))2 × C2 →(C2/−1)2 × C2,
(11.5)
and it is clear that the ﬁber of p over a point (0,0) × C2 is P1 × P1. The proof for the ﬁber over
Zσ,σ ′,σ ′′/G is similar, by identifying the blow-up with (Bl0(C2/−1))3 →(C2/−1)3 as germs.
Proof of Theorem 11.1. By Lemma 11.5, we can apply [dCM04, Corollary 2.3.9] to obtain the formula
(11.1) for relative motives. Pushing forward to a point, and noting that, by Remarks 11.3 and 11.4, the
isomorphism class of h(Wσ/G) (resp. h(Vσ,σ ′/G) ) does not depend on σ (resp. σ and σ′), we obtain
the isomorphism (11.2) of Chow motives.
11.1. Relation to orbifold motives

the deﬁnition of orbifold Chow motives, we refer to [FTV19] (based on constructions in [AGV02],
[AGV08], [JKK07]).

12. Beauville’s weak splitting conjecture
As a striking consequence of the hyper-Kummer construction, we can prove Beauville’s weak splitting
conjecture [Bea07] for all Kum3-type hyper-Kähler varieties.
Let X be a projective hyper-Kähler variety. Consider the subalgebra R•(X) ⊂CH•(X)Q generated
by divisor classes. The following weak splitting conjecture was proposed by Beauville in [Bea07]:
Conjecture 12.1. For any hyper-Kähler variety X, the cycle class map cl: CH•(X)Q →H2•(X,Q) induces
an inclusion R•(X) ,→H2•(X,Q).
For K3 surfaces, the conjecture is a reformulation of results established by Beauville and Voisin
in their inﬂuential paper [BV04]. There are now many cases in which the conjecture is veriﬁed.
For example, it was proven by the second author for the generalized Kummer variety Kn(A) on
an abelian surface A ([Fu15]). For any hyper-Kähler variety X of one of the known deformation
types, the conjecture has been proven by Riess [Rie16] under the assumption that X admits a rational
Lagrangian ﬁbration. We will use her result together with the hyper-Kummer construction to prove
the following result.

Theorem 12.2. Let K be a projective hyper-Kähler sixfold of Kum3-type. Then Beauville’s weak splitting
conjecture holds for K.
Proof. By the aforementioned work of Riess [Rie16], Beauville’s conjecture holds for the hyper-
Kummer K3[3]-variety YK associated to K. Indeed, YK has Picard rank at least 17, and therefore
there exists an isotropic vector in NS(YK) by Meyer’s theorem [Mey84]. By [BM14], this implies the
existence of a Lagrangian ﬁbration Y ′ →P3 for a birational hyper-Kähler model Y ′ of YK, and so
Conjecture 12.1 holds for Y ′ by [Rie16], hence also holds for YK by [Rie14].

13. Homological motives, Hodge and Tate conjectures
The Kuga–Satake construction ([KS67, Del71]) produces an abelian variety from the Hodge structure
on the second cohomology of any hyper-Kähler variety. It is generally expected that the motive of
any hyper-Kähler variety is controlled by the motive of the associated Kuga–Satake abelian variety.
We make this precise via the following conjecture.
Conjecture 13.1. Let X be a projective hyper-Kähler variety, and let KS(X) be the associated Kuga–Satake
abelian variety. Then X is motivated by KS(X), i.e., the motive of X belongs to the thick tensor subcategory
of motives generated by the motive of KS(X).


## Page 86


86
S. Floccari, L. Fu
86
S. Floccari, L. Fu
The conjecture depends on the choice of a category of motives. If we use André motives [And96b],
Conjecture 13.1 is known for K3 surfaces by André [And96a], and for all hyper-Kähler varieties of
known deformation types by [Sol21a] and [FFZ21]. The strongest version of Conjecture 13.1 is in the
category of Chow motives; Theorem 9.7 conﬁrms the conjecture for the rational Chow motive of
super-Kummer K3 surfaces. In this section, we instead investigate Conjecture 13.1 in the category
of homological motives. Although weaker than its Chow-theoretic version, the conjecture is already
wide open in this homological setting. In the sequel, Mot is the category of rational homological
motives with respect to singular cohomology, and h is the contravariant functor sending a smooth
projective variety to its homological motive.
We will show that Conjecture 13.1 holds for the homological motives of all of the varieties involved
in the hyper-Kummer construction and the Mongardi–Rapagnetta–Saccà double cover construction.
In fact, we have the following result, which is based on several previous works.
Theorem 13.2 ([Flo24, FV24, Flo23, FF26]). Consider the following hyper-Kähler varieties:
(i) any K3 surface or K3[n]-type variety satisfying the following condition: there exists an isometric
embedding of rational quadratic spaces H2
tr(X,Q) ,→U⊕3
Q ⊕⟨−a⟩Q, for some positive integer a.
(ii) any variety of Kum2-type;
(iii) any variety of Kum3-type;
(iv) any OG6-resolution.
Then the Kuga–Satake variety KS(X) of X is isogenous to a power of an abelian fourfold of Weil type with
discriminant 1, and Conjecture 13.1 holds for the homological motive of X, that is, h(X) belongs to the thick
tensor subcategory of homological motives generated by the motive of KS(X).
Proof. (i). For a K3 surface S satisfying the condition in (i), the conclusion follows from [Flo26,
Theorem 5.11]. If X is a K3[n]-type variety as in (i), then X is birational to a smooth and projective
moduli space on a K3 surface S as above, by [Add16]. The Kuga–Satake variety of X is isogenous to
that of S. By [Rie14, Bül20], X is motivated by S, and therefore Conjecture 13.1 holds for X as well.
(ii). This is a consequence of [FV24]. Let K be any variety of Kumn-type and let A be its inter-

1, the Kuga–Satake variety of K is isogenous to A4, and the Kuga–Satake–Hodge conjecture holds
for K. Moreover, there exists an algebraic cycle γ inducing the canonical isomorphism of Hodge
structures between H1(A,Q) and H3(K,Q).

the homological motive of K decomposes into Künneth components h(K) =

13.1. Hodge conjecture
Theorem 13.2 has very strong consequences for the Hodge conjecture of the varieties considered in
this paper. Following Varesco [Var23, Deﬁnition 1.2], we say that two hyper-Kähler varieties X and
X′ are Hodge similar if there is a Hodge similarity between H2
tr(X,Q) and H2
tr(X′,Q), that is, an
isomorphism of Hodge structures which rescales the form by some scalar factor.
Corollary 13.4. Let X1,··· ,Xn be any collection of hyper-Kähler varieties as in Theorem 13.2 such that the
Xi are Hodge similar to each other. Then the Hodge conjecture holds for all products of powers of X1,··· ,Xn
as well as of their Kuga–Satake varieties. In particular, the Hodge conjecture holds for all powers of any
hyper-Kähler variety X as in Theorem 13.2.
Proof. By [Var23, Proposition 0.2], all the Kuga–Satake varieties KS(Xi) have the same isogeny fac-
tors. In fact, each of the Kuga–Satake varieties KS(Xi) is isogenous to some power of the same
abelian fourfold A, which is of Weil type with discriminant 1. Therefore, the tensor subcategories
⟨h(KS(Xi))⟩⊗
Mot of Mot all coincide with ⟨h(A)⟩⊗
Mot. The Hodge conjecture has been established for
any abelian fourfold of Weil type with discriminant 1 [Mar22] and all its powers [Flo26] (see also
[FF26] for an alternative proof); equivalently, the Hodge realization functor R: ⟨h(A)⟩⊗
Mot →HSQ is
full. Let Y be a product of powers of the Xi and their Kuga–Satake varieties as in the statement. The-
orem 13.2 implies that h(Y ) ∈⟨h(A)⟩⊗
Mot. Hence, the restriction of the functor R to the subcategory
⟨h(Y )⟩⊗
Mot is full; in other words, the Hodge conjecture holds for (all powers of) Y .
Remark 13.5. Corollary 13.4 implies the Shafarevich conjecture for varieties in Theorem 13.2: any
Hodge similitude φ: H2
tr(X,Q) →H2
tr(Y ,Q) between hyper-Kähler varieties X,Y as in Theorem 13.2
is algebraic. Some cases of Shafarevich conjecture were proven in [Bus19, Huy19, Mar24, Var23,
Flo26, O’G26].
As another interesting consequence of Corollary 13.4, we observe the following Torelli-type result:
Corollary 13.6. Let X and X′ be deformation equivalent projective hyper-Kähler manifolds such that
H2(X,Q) and H2(X′,Q) are Hodge isometric. Assume that X and X′ are both as in Theorem 13.2. Then
their homological motives are isomorphic as Frobenius algebra objects in Mot:
(h(X),∪) ≃(h(X′),∪).
(13.2)
Proof. We can apply [Sol21b] or [Flo22, Theorem 4.7] to obtain an isomorphism of rational cohomol-
ogy algebras f : H∗(X,Q)
≃−→H∗(X′,Q) such that it moreover respects the class of a point, hence is
Frobenius. Then the Hodge conjecture for X ×X′, established in Corollary 13.4, allows us to conclude
that f and f −1 are both algebraic.
Remark 13.7. By Taelman [Tae23, Theorems 4.10 and 4.11], if two hyper-Kähler varieties X, X′ of
known deformation type are derived equivalent, then H2(X,Q) and H2(X′,Q) are Hodge isometric.
Therefore, Corollary 13.6 applies to X and X′ as in Theorem 13.2 that are Fourier–Mukai partners,
hence provides new instances of the so-called multiplicative Orlov conjecture proposed by Fu–Vial
[FV21, Conjecture 4.7] for homological motives; see [MSY26] for another recent result.
Remark 13.8. In Corollary 13.6, if X and X′ are deﬁned over a subﬁeld F ⊂C, then there exists a

holds in the category of homological motives over F′. In particular, there is a Gal(F/F)-equivariant

A powerful strategy towards the Tate conjecture (in characteristic zero) is to reduce it to the
Hodge conjecture via the following Mumford–Tate conjecture (see e.g. [Moo17b]):
Conjecture 13.9 (Mumford–Tate conjecture). Let X be a smooth projective variety deﬁned over a ﬁnitely
generated subﬁeld F ⊂C. Then under the identiﬁcation GL(H∗(Xan,Q) ⊗Qℓ) ≃GL(H∗
´et(XF,Qℓ)) pro-

where MT(X) ⊂GL(H∗(Xan,Q)) is the Mumford–Tate group of X and Gℓ(X)◦is the identity component
of the Zariski closure of the ℓ-adic Galois representation.
Corollary 13.10. Let F be a ﬁnitely generated subﬁeld of C and let ℓbe a prime number. Let X1,··· ,Xn
be smooth projective F-varieties such that their associated complex varieties are as in Theorem 13.2 and are
Hodge similar to each other. Then the Tate conjecture holds for all products of powers of Xi’s. In particular,
the Tate conjecture holds for all powers of any F-variety X with XC as in Theorem 13.2.
Proof. The proof is similar to [FF26, Corollary 5.12]. We include it for completeness. For a variety
X satisfying the Mumford–Tate conjecture, the Tate conjecture for (powers of) X is equivalent to the
Hodge conjecture for (powers of) XC; see [Moo17a, Proposition 2.3.2]. By the cases of the Hodge
conjecture established in Corollary 13.4, it suﬃces to show the Mumford–Tate conjecture for all
products of powers of Xi. As all the varieties in Theorem 13.2 are motivated by abelian varieties,
we can invoke Commelin’s result [Com19, Theorem 10.3] to reduce to showing the Mumford–Tate
conjecture for each individual variety in the list of Theorem 13.2. The Mumford–Tate conjecture for
K3 surfaces is proved in [And96a], while in [FFZ21, Theorem 1.18], we proved the Mumford–Tate
conjecture for all hyper-Kähler varieties of known deformation types.
A. Lattices
We collect in this appendix some results about lattices following Nikulin [Nik80], and carry out some
computations which are relevant to the main body of the paper.
4We include here the prediction on the semisimplicity, which is sometimes referred to as the Grothendieck–
Serre conjecture in the literature; see for example [Jan90, Conjecture 12.5].


## Page 90


90
S. Floccari, L. Fu
90
S. Floccari, L. Fu
A lattice L is a free Z-module of ﬁnite rank, denoted by rk(L), equipped with a non-degenerate
integer-valued symmetric bilinear form, which we often denote by (a,b)L or just (a,b). The associated
quadratic form on L ⊗Z R can be diagonalized with t+ entries equal to 1 and t−entries equal to −1,
and the signature of the lattice L is (t+,t−); we have rk(L) = t++t−. If L is a lattice and k is a non-zero
integer, we let L(k) be the lattice obtained from L by multiplying the bilinear form by a factor k. A
lattice is called even if (v,v) ∈2Z for any v ∈L, and odd otherwise; all the lattices considered in this
paper are even. We denote by O(L) the group of orthogonal transformations of the lattice L; for any
k ∈Z, the orthogonal group O(L(k)) is canonically identiﬁed with O(L).
The dual lattice of L is L∨B HomZ(L,Z). The bilinear form gives an embedding with ﬁnite
cokernel L →L∨, sending x to (x,−); moreover, L∨is naturally equipped with a Q-valued bilinear
form. Thus AL B L∨/L is a ﬁnite abelian group, called the discriminant group of L; a lattice is
called unimodular if AL is trivial. For any even lattice L there is a non-degenerate quadratic form qL
on AL with values in Q/2Z, i.e. qL : AL →Q/2Z is such that qL(na) = n2qL(a) for a ∈AL, n ∈Z.
Denoting by ¯w ∈AL the class of w ∈L∨, we have qL( ¯w) = (w,w)L∨modulo 2Z. We have an associated
symmetric bilinear form bL(a,b) = 1
2(qL(a + b) −qL(a) −qL(b)) on AL with values in Q/Z. We denote
by length(AL) the minimal number of generators of the abelian group AL, called the length of AL.
There is a natural action of O(L) on L∨, which induces a homomorphism O(L) →O(AL,qL), where
O(AL,qL) is the group of isometries of the discriminant group. By deﬁnition, the stable orthogonal
group eO(L) is the kernel of this homomorphism.
The signature and the discriminant form are the basic invariants of a lattice. An overlattice of
an even lattice L is a lattice M with an isometric embedding L ⊂M with ﬁnite index. The following
result is proved by Nikulin [Nik80].
Theorem A.1 ([Nik80, Proposition 1.4.1]). There is a bijection between even overlattices of L and isotropic
subgroups of the discriminant group AL.
The bijection sends an even overlattice L ⊂M to the isotropic subgroup H = M/L of AL. From
the chain of embeddings L ⊂M ⊂M∨⊂L∨one sees that AM = (M∨/L)/H, and in fact M∨/L =
H⊥⊂AL. It follows that |AM| =
|AL|
[M:L]2 , where [M : L] is the index of L in M.
A sublattice L ⊂M is saturated if M/L is torsion-free, i.e. mw ∈L implies w ∈L, for any w ∈M
and non-zero m ∈Z. An embedding L ,→M of lattices is called primitive if the image of L is
saturated; equivalently, if its cokernel is torsion free. Two primitive embeddings j,j′ : L →M are said
to be equivalent if there exist isometries f ∈O(L) and g ∈O(M) such that j′ = g ◦j ◦f .
Let L,M be even lattices and let j : L ,→M be a primitive embedding with orthogonal comple-
ment K. The embedding j corresponds to the datum of the gluing subgroup H := M/(L ⊕K) ⊂AL⊕K
which determines the overlattice M of L ⊕K. Since L and K are primitive in M, the projections of
H to AL and AK are injective, i.e., H is the graph of an anti-isometry γ : HL →HK for subgroups
HL, HK of AL, AK respectively. Nikulin [Nik80, Proposition 1.15.1] proves the following result, which
allows one to classify primitive embeddings of a given lattice L into another lattice M.
Theorem A.2. The primitive embeddings of an even lattice L with invariants (t+,t−,qL) into an even
lattice M with invariants (m+,m−,qM) are determined by the data (HL,HM,γ,K,γK) where:
1) HL is a subgroup of AL, HM is a subgroup of AM;
2) γ : HL →HM is an isomorphism such that qM(γ(x)) = qL(x) for all x ∈HL;

−−−→δ′ is the isometry induced by ( ¯φ−1,ξ) ∈O(AL ⊕AM(−1)).
We now recall some facts about isometries of lattices. The following result is again a consequence
of the work of Nikulin.
Theorem A.3 ([Nik80, Thm. 1.14.2 and Rmk. 1.14.5], [Huy16, Chapter 14, 1.5]). Let L be an even
indeﬁnite lattice. Assume that either:
(i) rk(L) ⩾length(AL) + 2, or
(ii) AL = (Z/2Z)3 ⊕A′ for some abelian group A′ and, for any odd prime p, the length of AL ⊗Z Zp is
at most rk(L) −2.
Then the lattice L is uniquely determined by its signature and discriminant form, and the natural map
O(L) →O(AL,qL) is surjective.
Remark A.4. By [Nik80, Thm. 1.14.2], the hypothesis in the above theorem may be further relaxed
as follows: for any odd prime p, the length of AL ⊗Z Zp is at most rk(L) −2 and, if the length of
AL ⊗Z Z2 = rk(L), then AL contains a copy AU(2), where U(2) is a hyperbolic plane with the form
multiplied by 2.
Combining this theorem with Theorem A.2, we obtain the following criterion for the uniqueness
of primitive embeddings of a lattice into a unimodular lattice.
Theorem A.5. Let j : L ,→M be a primitive embedding of an even lattice L into an even unimodular
lattice M, and assume the complement of j(L) satisﬁes the assumption of Theorem A.3. Then all primitive
embeddings of L in M are equivalent to j.
Let L be a lattice and h ∈L. The divisibility div(h) is the largest integer such that (h,−)/div(h)
belongs to L∨, or, equivalently, the positive generator of the ideal (h,L) of Z. The following statement
is originally due to Eichler.
Theorem A.6 ([GHS10, Lemma 3.5]). Let L be a lattice containing at least two orthogonal hyperbolic
planes. Let h ∈L be a primitive class, of square 2d and divisibility γ. The eO(L)-orbit of h consists precisely
of those primitive vectors h′ of square 2d, divisibility γ and such that [(h,−)/γ] = [(h′,−)/γ] in AL.

Finally, let M be an overlattice of L, corresponding to an isotropic subgroup H of AL. Then
an isometry f of L extends to an isometry of M if and only if the action ¯f on AL satisﬁes ¯f (H) =
H. If L ,→M is a primitive embedding with orthogonal complement K, let H ⊂AL ⊕AK be the
corresponding gluing subgroup; recall that H is the graph of an anti-isometry γ : HL →HK, for
subgroups HL,HK of AL,AK respectively. Then f ∈O(L) can be extended (non-uniquely) to an
isometry ef ∈O(M) if and only if the induced action ¯f of f on AL stabilizes HL and there exists an
isometry g ∈O(K) whose action on AK stabilizes HK and satisﬁes ¯g|HK = γ ◦¯f|HL ◦γ−1.
A.1. Some examples
We will now discuss some lattices which play a role in the paper. The hyperbolic plane U is the
rank 2 lattice of signature (1,1) generated by isotropic classes e,f such that (e,f ) = 1. It has the
property that any embedding U ,→L into an even lattice L is automatically saturated and L splits as
the orthogonal direct sum L = U ⊕U⊥. We shall illustrate the use of Theorem A.2 to understand the
orbits of primitive vectors in lattices of the form U(2)⊕k ⊕U.
Lemma A.7. Let M = U(2)⊕k ⊕U, for some k ⩾1. Let d , 0 be an integer. If d is odd, there is a single
O(M)-orbit of primitive vectors of square 2d, and any such necessarily has divisibility 1. If d is even, there
are exactly two O(M)-orbits of primitive vectors of square 2d: the orbit of vectors with divisibility 1 and
that of vectors with divisibility 2. For a primitive class h of square 2d, we have h⊥= U(2)⊕k ⊕⟨−2d⟩if
div(h) = 1 and h⊥= U(2)⊕k−1 ⊕U ⊕⟨−2d⟩if div(h) = 2.
Proof. The discriminant group A⟨2d⟩is isomorphic to Z/2dZ, with form k 7→k2/2d ∈Q/2Z. On
the other hand, AM = AU(2)⊕k is the cokernel of U(2)⊕k ,→1
2U(2)⊕k; hence, AM = (Z/2Z)2k. Any
element in AM has square 1 or 0, and there are 3 orbits under O(AM,qM): the orbit of 0, that of
elements where the quadratic form takes value 1 and that of non-zero elements where the form takes
value 0. By Theorem A.3, the homomorphism O(M) →O(AM,qM) is surjective.
We describe the equivalence classes of primitive embeddings of the lattice L B ⟨2d⟩into M via
Theorem A.2. They are determined by the sets (HL,HM,γ,K,γK). Since AM  (Z/2Z)2k, we must
have HM = 0 or HM  Z/2Z. But AL contains a unique subgroup isomorphic to Z/2Z, and the
quadratic form takes value d/2 ∈Q/2Z on its generator. If d is odd, this subgroup is not isometric to
any subgroup of AM and therefore HL = HM = 0. We then get a unique equivalence class of primitive
embeddings of ⟨2d⟩into M; the image must be generated by a primitive class h of divisibility 1, and
we have h⊥= U(2)⊕k ⊕⟨−2d⟩.
If d is even, in the same way we get a single orbit of primitive vectors h of square 2d and
divisibility 1, with h⊥= U(2)⊕k ⊕⟨−2d⟩. But in this case d/2 ∈Q/2Z is 0 or 1 according to whether
d is divisible by 4 or not. Hence, there exist other primitive embeddings with HL  Z/2Z and
choosing a subgroup HM generated by an element on which the form takes value 0 or 1, respectively.
As there is a single orbit of such vectors under O(AM,qM), the discriminant form of K is uniquely
determined and is isomorphic to AU(2)⊕k−1⊕⟨−2d⟩, which has length 2k −1. Since rk(K) = 2k + 1, the
lattice K is uniquely determined by its signature and discriminant form, by Theorem A.3. Therefore,
K = U(2)⊕k−1 ⊕U ⊕⟨−2d⟩.
Moreover, O(K) →O(AK) is surjective, and therefore all primitive
embeddings L ,→M with HL  Z/2Z are equivalent to each other. The images of the generator of
L via these embeddings give the unique O(M)-orbit of primitive vectors of square 2d (with d even)
and divisibility 2.

The Kummer lattice appears in [Nik75] and [PSS71]. We recall some of its properties following [Huy16,

Example A.9. Let A be a 2-dimensional complex torus, and let Km(A) denote the associated Kummer
K3 surface, which is the minimal resolution of A/ ± 1. Let R1,...,R16 be the 16 disjoint smooth
rational curves which are the exceptional divisors of Km(A) →A/ ±1. Their cohomology classes ri ∈
H2(Km(A),Z) are pairwise orthogonal (−2)-classes, and the saturation of the sublattice generated
by the ri is the Kummer lattice LKm. The orthogonal complement of LKm in ΛK3 is isometric to the
lattice U(2)⊕3, which is the image of the push-forward π∗: H2(A,Z) →H2(Km(A),Z) induced by
the degree-2 rational map π: A 99K Km(A). Therefore ALKm is isometric to AU(2)⊕3 ≃(Z/2Z)6.
Remark A.10. Notice that U(2)⊕3 satisﬁes the assumptions of Theorem A.3. Hence, there exists a
unique equivalence class of primitive embeddings LKm ,→ΛK3, by Theorem A.5. Similarly, there
exists a unique equivalence class of primitive embeddings LKm ,→eΛK3; the orthogonal complement
is U(2)⊕3 ⊕U in this case.

This leads to the characterization of Kummer K3 surfaces as those K3 surfaces containing a
primitive copy of LKm in their Néron–Severi lattice. However, there are 2 equivalence classes of
primitive embeddings of LKm in ΛK3[n] when n is odd.
Lemma A.11. If n is even, there exists a unique equivalence class of primitive embeddings j : LKm ,→
ΛK3[n], and we have im(j)⊥= U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−2n + 2⟩. If n is odd, there exist 2 equivalence classes of
primitive embeddings j : LKm ,→ΛK3[n]; for one we have im(j)⊥= U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−2n + 2⟩and for the other
we have im(j)⊥= U(2)⊕2 ⊕U ⊕⟨−2n + 2⟩.
Proof. Let L B LKm and M B ΛK3[n]. We determine the possibilities for the sets (HL,HM,γ,K,γK)
of Theorem A.2. By the description of qL and qM, it is clear that either HL is trivial or HL  Z/2Z.
Then HM is either trivial or the unique order 2 subgroup of AM; the form qM takes value −(n−1)/2
on the generator. If HL = HM = 0, we get an equivalence class of primitive embeddings of LKm in
ΛK3[n]; the orthogonal complement is U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−2(n −1)⟩. If n −1 is odd in fact we must have
HL = HM = 0, and this is the only possibility. If n −1 is even, we may also have that HL  Z/2Z is
non-trivial, and qL takes value 0 or 1 on the generator of HL according to whether n−1 is divisible by
4 or not. All such subgroups are conjugated to each other via isometries of L ([GS16, Remark 2.3(5)]).
Embedding ΛK3[n] in eΛK3 with complement generated by a primitive vector v of square 2(n−1) and
applying Lemma A.7, the orthogonal complement of such a primitive embedding LKm ,→ΛK3[n] must
be isometric to K = U(2)⊕2⊕U⊕⟨−2(n−1)⟩. Since O(K) →O(AK,qK) is surjective by Theorem A.3,
these embeddings form a unique equivalence class.
A.3. The Barnes–Wall lattice
There is another rank 16 negative deﬁnite lattice which plays a role in our paper, the Barnes–Wall
lattice BW16.
Deﬁnition A.12. The Barnes–Wall lattice BW16 is the unique negative deﬁnite lattice of rank 16
with discriminant form isomorphic to AU(2)⊕4 and which contains no (−2)-classes.
The above properties indeed determine uniquely the Barnes–Wall lattice, see [SV94].
Remark A.13. The discriminant group ABW16 = AU(2)4 is identiﬁed with a quadratic space of di-
mension 8 over F2 which is the sum of 4 planes with form

1 0

, and O(ABW16,qBW16) is iden-
tiﬁed with the orthogonal group O+
8(2).
By [CS98, Chapter 4, 10], the natural homomorphism
O(BW16) →O(ABW16,qABW16) is surjective.
It follows that there are 3 orbits for the action of
O(BW16) on the discriminant group ABW16 = AU(2)⊕4: the orbit of 0, the orbit of non-trivial ele-
ments on which the form takes value 0 and that of elements on which the form takes value 1.
The Barnes–Wall lattice does not embed primitively in the K3 lattice, as the complement would
have to be a lattice of rank 6 and discriminant of length 8, which is impossible. However, BW16 can
be embedded primitively in the Mukai lattice.
Lemma A.14. There exists a unique equivalence class of primitive embeddings BW16 ,→eΛK3.
The
orthogonal complement is isometric to U(2)⊕4.
Proof. For the existence of such a primitive embedding we may appeal to [Nik80, Corollary 1.12.3].
The uniqueness follows from Theorem A.5.

Lemma A.15. The Barnes–Wall lattice does not embed in ΛK3[n] if n is even. For any odd n ⩾3, there
exists a unique equivalence class of primitive embeddings BW16 ,→ΛK3[n]. The orthogonal complement is
isometric to U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−2(n −1)⟩.
Proof. Let j : BW16 ,→eΛK3 be a primitive embedding. Primitive embeddings of BW16 in ΛK3[n]
correspond to the choice of a primitive vector v in im(j)⊥of square 2(n−1), which gives j′ : BW16 ,→
v⊥= ΛK3[n]. Since im(j)⊥= U(2)⊕4, no such vector exists if n −1 is odd; moreover, by Theorem
A.6 applied to U⊕4, primitive vectors of ﬁxed square in im(j)⊥form a single O(U(2)⊕4)-orbit. Since
O(BW16) →O(ABW16,qABW16) is surjective, any isometry of im(j)⊥extends to an isometry of eΛK3.
Hence, for n odd, all primitive embeddings j′ : BW16 ,→ΛK3[n] are equivalent to each other; the
orthogonal complement im(j′)⊥⊂ΛK3[n] is necessarily isometric to U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−2(n −1)⟩.
Remark A.16. Let j : BW16 ,→eΛK3 be a primitive embedding and let v ∈im(j)⊥be a primitive class
of square 4d, for some d > 0. Then the saturation L of the sublattice generated by im(j) and v is
an overlattice of index 2 of BW16 ⊕⟨v⟩, isometric to LKm ⊕⟨4d⟩. In fact, L is the unique lattice
of signature (1,16) and discriminant form isomorphic to (AU(2)⊕3⊕⟨−4d⟩)(−1), but also LKm ⊕⟨4d⟩
has these properties. In particular, for any d > 0, there exists a primitive embedding of BW16 in
LKm ⊕⟨4d⟩.

summand. We can write h = αh′ +βξ, for a primitive class h′ ∈U⊕3 and coprime integers α,β. Then
div(h) = g.c.d.(α,2d).
Indeed, let t B g.c.d(α,2d), and write α = tα′, 2d = td′. Then, for a class l = al′ +bξ with l′ in U⊕3,

and, therefore, there exist integers a0,b0 such that α′ · a0 −d′β · b0 = 1. Choosing g ∈U⊕3 such that
(h′,g) = 1 we ﬁnd (h,a0g + b0ξ) = t.

Lemma A.20. Let h ∈L be a primitive class of square 2d and divisibility γ. Then:
(i) if γ = 1, then ˆh ∈ˆL is a primitive class of divisibility 1 and square 2d;
(ii) if γ = 2, then ˆh ∈ˆL equals 2 · ˆh′ for a primitive class ˆh′ ∈ˆL of divisibility 1 and square d/2;
(iii) if γ > 2, then ˆh ∈ˆL equals 2 · ˆh′ for a primitive class ˆh′ ∈ˆL of divisibility 2 and square d/2.

is suﬃcient to determine the properties of ˆh for a single representative in each O(L)-orbit. We choose
the basis ei,fi, i = 1,2,3, ξ of L as in the proof of Lemma A.19, so that, moreover, ˆei, ˆfi, i = 1,2,3,
and ˆξ/2 form a basis of ˆL. Choosing an explicit representative h as in the various cases of Lemma
A.19, it is easy to verify the claimed properties of ˆh.
While any isometry of L extends to an isometry of the overlattice ˆL, there are isometries of ˆL
which do not stabilize the sublattice L.
Lemma A.21. Let L = U⊕3 ⊕⟨−8⟩and let ˆL = U⊕3 ⊕⟨−2⟩. Let δ ∈ˆL be a primitive class of square
−2 and divisibility 2 such that L = δ⊥⊕⟨2δ⟩. Let f ∈O(ˆL); we have f (δ) = 2αu + βδ for a primitive
u ∈δ⊥and coprime integers α,β with β odd. Then f lies in O(L) ⊂O(ˆL) if and only if α is even.
Proof. Primitive vectors of square −2 and divisibility 2 in ˆL form a single orbit under the orthogonal
group, and once such a vector δ is ﬁxed, any other is written as 2αu + βδ, for a primitive u ∈δ⊥of
square 2e and coprime integers α,β with β odd and such that moreover 4α2e −β2 = −1. Hence, any
isometry f ∈O(ˆL) satisﬁes f (δ) = 2αu + βδ for some u,α,β as above.
Assume that f ∈O(L). Then α must be even. Then the class ξ B 2δ in L is primitive of square
−8 and divisibility 8; such vectors form a single orbit under O(L), and any other such vector is written

Conversely, if f (δ) = 2αu + βδ with α even, there exists some g ∈O(L) such that g(δ) = f (δ).
Hence, g−1f (δ) = δ, so clearly g−1f , and therefore also f , belongs to O(L).

A.5. A few other lattices
We collect here some information about a few more lattices appearing in our paper. Consider again
LKm as an overlattice of
L16
i=1 Z · ri where the ri are pairwise orthogonal classes of square −2.
Deﬁnition A.28. We denote by NS the sublattice ⟨ri + rj⟩⊥⊂LKm, for some i , j.
Lemma A.29. The lattice NS does not depend on the choice of i and j. This lattice is negative deﬁnite of
rank 15, and its discriminant group ANS is isomorphic to (Z/2Z)6⊕Z/4Z. Moreover, there exists a unique
equivalence class of primitive embeddings of NS into ΛK3, with orthogonal complement U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−4⟩.
Proof. For any other l , k, there exists an isometry of LKm sending ri to rl and rj to rk. Indeed,
there is a natural (Z/2Z)4-action on LKm via translations, which is transitive on the 16 classes rm.
We may thus assume that ri = rl. We identify the set of points rm with a 4-dimensional vector space
V over F2, in such a way that ri corresponds to the origin. Now any (aﬃne) linear transformation of
V induces an isometry of LKm, as it sends aﬃne hyperplanes to aﬃne hyperplanes. Notice that this
isometry will ﬁx the origin of V . But we can surely ﬁnd such a linear transformation sending rj to
rk, whenever rj and rk are not the origin of V . We thus have found an isometry of LKm sending ri to
rl and rj to rk. This shows that the lattice NS does not depend on the choice of i and j. It is clear
that NS is a negative deﬁnite lattice of rank 15.
We consider now the Kummer surface Km(A) on an abelian surface A; then LKm is primitively
embedded into H2(Km(A),Z) and the composition NS ,→LKm ,→H2(Km(A),Z) gives a primi-
tive embedding of NS into the K3 lattice ΛK3. The orthogonal complement is the saturation of
H2(A,Z)(2)⊕⟨ri +rj⟩. But this lattice is already saturated: identifying again the rm with the points of
a 4-dimensional F2-vector space V , there exists an aﬃne hyperplane W in V containing ri but not rj;
if v = αu+β(ri +rj) is a Q-linear combination with u ∈H2(A,Z)(2) which belongs to H2(Km(A),Z),
then (v, 1
2
P
k∈W rk) = −β must be an integer, and hence v ∈H2(A,Z)(2) ⊕⟨ri + rj⟩. This shows that
the orthogonal complement of NS in ΛK3 is isometric to U(2)⊕3 ⊕⟨−4⟩. Since ΛK3 is unimodular,
we conclude that ANS is the abelian group (Z/2Z)6 ⊕Z/4Z. Finally, the embedding of NS in ΛK3 is
unique up to isometries of ΛK3 by Theorem A.5.
Remark A.30. The lattice U ⊕NS is uniquely determined by its signature and discriminant form by
Theorem A.3. It is therefore isometric to LKm ⊕⟨4⟩. It follows that there exist primitive vectors v
and v′ of square 4 in NS ⊕U such that v⊥= LKm and (v′)⊥= BW16.
